#### Abstracts
##### **MemOnce: A Scalable Memory Architecture for Large Language Models**
2611.27973v1 by Chang Nie, Chaoyou Fu, Songlin Liu, Yiqing Hu, Caifeng Shan

Deploying large language models (LLMs) in long-horizon and continual interactions requires maintaining reusable memory states for future inference, posing challenges for existing architectures. Standard Transformers accumulate layer-wise, token-level KV caches, incurring linear GPU memory growth and quadratic computational complexity as the context expands. In contrast, linear-time architectures compress history into fixed-size states, but struggle to retain fine-grained details. We introduce MemOnce, a foundation architecture that learns scalable memory states for language models. MemOnce is based on two core designs: (a) Memory encoder-decoder: it caches only a small subset of final-layer hidden states as compact memory representations and dynamically decodes them back into KV caches for attention layers; (b) Memory sparse attention: for each continual input, MemOnce adaptively retrieves relevant memory representations, with the sparse indices computed only once across layers. Coupling these two designs enables efficient on-the-fly memory loading and KV cache decoding, drastically reducing the GPU memory footprint and achieving linear-time complexity. We conduct extensive experiments to show that MemOnce outperforms Transformers and other architectures across varying model sizes, context lengths, and training paradigms (pretraining and post-training adaptation). In particular, adapting Qwen3.5-4B with MemOnce reduces memory state cache by 1313$\times$ and prefill latency by 6.9$\times$ at 1M context, and improves throughput by 3.5$\times$ and overall memory performance by +5.77%. Finally, MemOnce generalizes zero-shot to downstream representation tasks, achieving competitive results against specialized models.

摘要：

##### **Non-penetrating World Models**
2611.27963v1 by Yuhang Dai, Yinwei Wu, Ziyue Lin, Han Ji, Xingyi Yang

We study a new problem of preventing object penetration in interactive video world models. To understand how existing models handle this, we introduce NoPeBench, a benchmark that evaluates how world models respond to physical obstacles. Our evaluation reveals that none of the open-source models can reliably prevent such failures. Surprisingly, however, these models already encode the information needed to predict such violations. We find that the hidden states of diffusion transformers (DiTs) can predict whether a candidate action will penetrate an obstacle. Building on this, we introduce NoPeWorld, a lightweight framework that detects and prevents penetration. Specifically, we train a small prediction head on top of a frozen DiT's hidden states to read out if a candidate action is safe or not. Unsafe actions are then replaced with safe ones that best preserve the original intent. Despite its simplicity, NoPeWorld substantially reduces the physical violation rate, averaged over six video world models, from 74% to 16%. Remarkably, our method shows zero-shot generalization to open-domain imagery, third-person viewpoints, and moving obstacles. We publicly release our code, the NoPeBench benchmark, and model weights. Project Page: https://nopeworld.github.io .

摘要：

##### **Model Carving: Training Transformers That Optimize Their Own Topology**
2611.27962v1 by Kaden Zheng, Pratyusha Sharma

Machine learning has largely kept separate the choice of architecture and the process of training. A classic Transformer, for instance, trains a fixed set of attention and MLP sublayers at every layer, in the same order, and optimizes the weights within. This uniformity is a convention rather than a principled design choice, and it is likely suboptimal: the best architecture for a given task may be smaller, more efficient, and far from uniform. We believe that, ideally, training itself should shape the model's topology. To this end, we introduce Model Carving, which learns both a Transformer's weights and its architecture in a single run. By applying learnable, scalar gates to each sublayer, we allow the training loss to remove the model's own components during training -- freeing a smaller heterogeneous network inside. At equal training compute, carved models reach a lower validation loss than dense models trained with up to 1.7 times more compute. Furthermore, carved models also outperform size-matched models produced by pruning or distillation, suggesting that the best way to obtain small models for edge devices is to carve them during training, not to compress a large one afterwards. This advantage both grows with scale and carries over to downstream tasks such as few-shot evaluation and generation. Because the removed sublayers no longer run, carved models also cost up to 34% fewer FLOPs per token and decode up to 1.6 times faster without cost in quality. Our results show that architecture, like weights, should be learned and not fixed in advance, and that inside every large transformer is a smaller, faster, stronger one waiting to be carved out.

##### **Decision Columns as an Index: Interpretable Embeddings for Explainable Entity Matching and Search**

Business data systems must determine whether records refer to the same entity and retrieve records satisfying requests whose decisive properties are implicit. ##### **Speculative Decoding Is Less Speculative Than You Think**
2611.27891v1 by Guanning Zeng, Andrea Zanette

Speculative decoding accelerates language-model inference by preparing candidate continuations before the target model generates them. Its efficiency depends on how well limited drafting computation anticipates the path the target will actually follow. Anticipating this path involves two sources of uncertainty: the target model's predictions and the randomness of sampling. Yet the former requires a target-model forward pass, while the latter can be fixed before drafting. Building on this observation, we introduce DICE (Draw-Informed Candidate Selection), which pre-samples and synchronizes verification randomness across drafting and verification. By combining these known draws with predictions from an existing drafter, DICE allocates computation toward continuations more likely to be used in the current sampling run. This principle guides node selection throughout draft-tree construction and extends to asynchronous prefetching, where DICE predicts which next-round drafts to prepare. Without additional drafter training or changes to the target sampling distribution, DICE achieves state-of-the-art decoding throughput across diverse settings, with speedups of up to 24.6pp over strong baselines.

##### **DistDNA: Language Model Fingerprinting under Stochastic Decoding**

Behavioral fingerprints identify a large language model (LLM) from its responses to a shared set of prompts, enabling applications such as verifying whether a deployed model corresponds to the checkpoint it claims to be. Held-out known seizure types from unseen subjects are detected only slightly above chance (AUROC 0.54-0.58 for all scorers). Per-subject rejection rates are highly uneven for all scorers, and GHOST does not significantly improve per-subject fairness over energy scoring (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank, p >= 0.25). We release the protocol, subject splits, and code as an initial baseline; our results indicate that open-set seizure type recognition remains a hard, open problem.

##### **Search-RSI: Expanding Search Worlds Along Multimodal Agents' Capability Frontier**
2611.27780v1 by Chengchao Lv, Yin Wang, Chunzheng Zhu, Wanli Chang

Multimodal search agents answer knowledge intensive visual questions by grounding them in retrieved evidence, and recent recipes train them on multi hop tasks that are synthesized from knowledge graphs and grown online to keep pace with the agent. These recipes, however, treat the searchable world as fixed: a new question can be well formed and verifiable while its evidence is absent from the retrieval backend, so the agent issues the right query, retrieves nothing, and is penalized as if its policy were at fault. Such unreachable negatives accumulate with every round of growth. We propose Search-RSI, a recursive self improvement framework that expands the search world along the agent's capability frontier. In each round, the current policy diagnoses its partially mastered regions of a task space spanned by hop depth, relation rarity, and distractor density; new tasks there are grown together with their evidence; and the same policy decides how they are used: candidates on which its probes disagree feed reinforcement learning, while those beyond its reach become hint guided demonstrations of its own. Keeping the policy inside this loop matters: an improver frozen at the start of training loses 3.2 points. On nine multimodal search benchmarks, Search-RSI keeps improving where static task growth saturates, raising the average accuracy of the recipe it builds on by 9.8 and 9.5 points at 9B and 27B, of which 7.0 points are out of reach for a world that is merely made consistent. The loop uses no external model and only privileged task structure that is removed before training, at 1.27x the compute of static growth.

##### **QuASAR: Toward an LLM-Assisted Framework for Screening, Prototyping, and Assessing Quantum-Advantage Opportunities in Scientific Computing**
2611.27778v1 by Mark L. Carroll, Jordan A. Caraballo-Vega, Brian P. Powell, John L. Schnase

Identifying scientific computations that could benefit from quantum algorithms requires both domain knowledge and fluency in quantum algorithm design, expertise that rarely resides in one person. We report results from QuASAR, an experimental, two-agent, LLM-driven pipeline intended to bridge this gap. One agent screens scientific literature for computational kernels with a plausible quantum-algorithmic analogue and proposes candidates, each backed by citations. A second agent independently verifies those citations. For candidates that survive verification, it reconstructs the source's computational structure, formulates a quantum mapping, builds and executes a prototype, and benchmarks it against classical baselines. We applied this pipeline to ten candidate computations drawn from the astrophysics literature. Nine candidates were carried through to a small-scale, benchmarked prototype. We report three in detail, chosen as the clearest examples with the least redundancy across the set. They span combinatorial optimization, stochastic simulation, and Bayesian inference. The pipeline attempted to supply quantum-design expertise a domain scientist might lack, reconstructing source algorithms where one existed and building original, disclosed mappings where none did. None of the three case studies achieved quantum advantage at the tested scales, and their astrophysical framing has not yet undergone expert validation. What the experiment does show is that the pipeline can turn a published idea into a runnable prototype, which a scientist can then inspect, test against classical methods, and decide whether to pursue. QuASAR is an exploratory framework, offered to the community for evaluation, critique, and further development, with an approach that could broaden the use of quantum computing in scientific research and extend to computational improvements more generally.

##### **Enhancing Attention with Higher-Order Attention**
2611.27764v1 by R. I. Cukier

Current decoder large language models rely on a combination of attention, feed forward and residual layers. The attention used is autoregressive (AR) though this is not strictly correct, as we note. That motivates the introduction of other attention methods that, while also not strictly AR, do enhance the AR character. One is based on a decomposition of mutual information (MI) into a set of Conditional MIs (CMIs) that correlate key tokens with pairs of query tokens. A second, higher-order attention, introduces correlations between contiguous key and query tokens. A third introduces correlations between a set of key and query tokens and uses a Generalized Maximum Entropy Principle to obtain an attention expression spanning a sequence of tokens. All these methods may enhance the information content of the autoregressive probabilities that are used in inference/generation. Applications to contrastive decoding and a systematic analysis of autoregressive probabilities are presented.

##### **Tasklets: A Unit-Test Benchmark for Language Model Architectures**
2611.27607v1 by Zayd Muhammad Kawakibi Zuhri, Munachiso Samuel Nwadike, Kentaro Inui, Alham Fikri Aji

We introduce Tasklets, a benchmark of small synthetic tasks for evaluating the computational capabilities of language model sequence-mixing layers. These tasks probe foundational capabilities such as recall, sequence manipulation, and state tracking. Such tasks are widely used in architecture research, but differences in task selection, formulation, and evaluation make comparisons across studies difficult. Tasklets brings these tasks together, organizing them by the capabilities they test and providing a shared setup for evaluating how different architectural layer types learn fundamental computations. We compare a broad range of layer types through controlled experiments with small models. Our task implementations and evaluation code support reproducible, low-cost testing of new architectural proposals: https://github.com/zaydzuhri/flame/tree/tasklets.

##### **PromptTTSD: Controllable Text to Spoken Dialogue Synthesis with Open-Ended Text Descriptions**
2611.27601v1 by Sulong Yang, Zhi Zeng, Xiupeng Wu, Juewen Hu, Pring Wong, Shuwu Zhang, Shuo Chen

Spoken dialogue synthesis aims to generate multi-turn conversational speech from dialogue scripts while maintaining speaker consistency and contextual appropriateness. However, existing models largely lack fine-grained controllability, which is critical for capturing the dynamic, context-dependent style shifts in natural conversations. While prompt-based TTS has enabled such control for single-speaker speech, existing datasets heavily rely on predefined tags, which fundamentally limits their capacity to capture open-ended styles. To address this gap, we propose a scalable pipeline for extracting conversational speech from multi-narrator audiobooks, featuring an improved two-stage alignment algorithm to preserve dialogue continuity, paired with LLM-based extraction of style descriptions directly from narrative text. We release PromptDialog, a large-scale multi-speaker conversational speech dataset comprising 8,383 hours of Chinese speech and 120 hours of English speech, with open-ended style descriptions for 79% of utterances. We further introduce PromptTTSD, a spoken dialogue synthesis model built via a prompt-aware fine-tuning strategy that integrates style prompts into text-speech interleaved sequences. PromptTTSD is an open-source spoken dialogue synthesis model that supports both zero-shot voice cloning and natural language style control. Experiments demonstrate that PromptTTSD not only maintains strong fundamental synthesis capabilities but also achieves competitive open-ended style control compared with proprietary models, and substantially improves contextual appropriateness in multi-turn dialogues.

##### **Can Language Models See the Circuit in Pixelated RFIC Passives?**
2611.27559v1 by Jonathan Zhou, Kaushik Sengupta

The emergence of inverse-designed, pixelated passives for microwave and millimeter-wave applications has been a major development in the field of RFIC design. These devices, which appear to be QR-code-like in structure, exist in a generalizable, high-dimensional design space that includes conventional passive topologies. However, these structures are naturally unintuitive for designers, and failures or deviations in performance are difficult to debug. In this work, we ask whether a frontier language model can understand, predict, and edit the responses and layout structure of pixelated passives. The agent, run with all tools disabled, is first asked to read layouts on 18x18 and 27x27 tile grids and, in a single reply, predict the input impedance and loss of the structure from 20-100 GHz. On eight GA-synthesized layouts and five random fills, the predicted Smith-chart locus follows full-wave simulation results closely, with a systematic shift that places features about 16% lower in frequency than EM. On multipath loop structures it identifies notches successfully but shows a smaller frequency bias, about half that of an ideal transmission-line model that ignores bends. The agent is then asked to debug seven designs that fail their specification, and given a budget of five EM simulations and at most twelve changed tiles, it is able to improve all seven and bring five designs within specification. These experiments suggest language models can serve as an interpretability and debugging tool for traditionally non-intuitive inverse-designed passives.

##### **Bringing AI Assistance to the Beznau Nuclear Power Plant: A Hierarchical System of LLM Agents for Reactor Monitoring**
2611.27556v1 by Wenqi Zhou, Leonardo Baldo, Edwin Kolbe, Manuel Arias Chao

Beznau Unit 1 in Switzerland, in operation since 1969, is the most experienced nuclear reactor still running worldwide. Five decades of incremental modernization have left its monitoring systems, alarm logs, variable catalogs, technical specifications and investigation reports in separate silos that were never designed to be queried together. As a result, investigating a core instrumentation anomaly means slow, manual gathering of knowledge scattered across databases, documents, and experienced colleagues. We present a hierarchical multi-agent assistant built on large language models (LLMs) to support these investigations and reduce the need for tedious manual searches in SAP systems. An orchestrating agent breaks down an engineer's question and hands the parts to specialized sub-agents, each with read-only access to one source. Domain expertise is written as natural-language procedures that plant engineers can read, review and revise. The system is strictly advisory, with every answer traced back to its sources. We also report organizational lessons from introducing agentic AI into a culture built on safety, traceability and conservative change. Beyond the technical architecture, the Beznau case study examines how an assistant can be introduced into a setting where evidence must be traceable, changes carefully reviewed, and operational decisions retained by plant personnel, paving the way for the responsible use of agentic AI in industrial plant monitoring.

##### **Vega-Fafnir: Is Greedy Decoding the Same Decoding? Where Rounding Noise in Speculative Verification Comes From and What Removing It Costs**
2611.27554v1 by Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Navin Kumar R

Serving engines treat the ways of running a greedy language model as interchangeable: cached or not, padded or not, alone or batched, one token or several verified at once (speculative decoding). In finite precision they round differently. We measure how often the greedy text of Qwen3-0.6B (bfloat16, 128 new tokens, GSM8K and ARC prompts) differs from a reference that decodes one token per pass. Verifying 4 guessed tokens per pass changes 60% of 150 prompts and padding changes 68%; every first divergence has a top-1 minus top-2 logit gap of 0.375 or less. A split of the noise shows that cache entries written by multi-token passes are about as noisy as the multi-token pass itself. A margin gate (fall back to one token when the gap is below a bound calibrated on separate GSM8K prompts) whose fallback reads that cache does not help: 58 of 100 prompts differ, against 61 of 100 for plain verification. Rebuilding the cache with one-token passes leaves 0 of 100 different but costs 7.2 model passes per token, against 1.0 for the reference. Fixed-width decoding makes drafted and plain decoding identical (0 of 100 prompts differ) at 0.75 passes per token. On shifted MMLU and HellaSwag prompts the rebuild gate leaves 0 of 60 different, plain verification 48 of 60. On 138 further and shifted prompts the bound was exceeded at 0.6% of positions and missed 0 of 309 flips. One GPU, one drafter, small models.

##### **Alliance Rupture and Repair in Simulating Cognitive Behavioral Therapy Dialogues**
2611.27544v1 by Jian Chai, Yitian Yang, Siqi Liu, Yi-Chia Wang, Yi-Chieh Lee

In cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), routine work can strain the client-counselor working alliance, and the counselor must then notice the rupture, repair it, and adapt the work that follows. Existing CBT simulators model client resistance and follow structured treatment plans, but rarely track alliance state or allow counselors to repair ruptures over multiple turns and revise their plans accordingly. We present ALLY, a simulation framework that brings alliance rupture, repair, and replanning into ongoing CBT dialogue. Each client has a hidden alliance state that governs how openly the client engages. Like a real counselor, the counselor agent sees only the dialogue. It must notice when the client pulls back or pushes against the work, repair the rupture, and replan the rest of the session around what it has learned. Automatic evaluation shows that ALLY's counselors are closer to real CBT counselors than those of existing public synthetic datasets in strategy flow and emotional dynamics, and that ALLY is rated highest overall in counseling quality (CTRS) and interpersonal skill (FIS-T). An expert evaluation with 20 licensed therapists also finds the simulated ruptures plausible and the repairs and replanning largely appropriate.

##### **On the Limits of Observability for Evaluating Coverage in Machine Unlearning**
2611.27543v1 by Ziluowen Luo, Jun Yin, Songyang Liu, Ji Qi, Chuang Hu, Kyrie You Wu, Chaozhuo Li, Senzhang Wang

Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of any requested subset of training data while preserving utility on the remaining data. Empirical evaluations measure predictive accuracy and infer training-set membership (MIAs) from model outputs or intermediate activations. However, by focusing on selected forget sets, these evaluations can overlook Coverage, the range of requests a method can fulfill. To assess this coverage, we first conduct a stress test of observability with 502 retrained models, finding that hidden embeddings reveal known deletions missed in predictions. To characterize capacity limits on coverage, we show that distinguishing all $n$ singleton deletions with linear scores can require a shared representation of rank $Ω(\log n)$. We further extend the bound to arbitrary subsets and show when partial deletion can appear complete. Building on this analysis, we propose CEO, a framework for evaluating unlearning coverage under limited observability. Its compositional color-shape benchmark makes forgetting and retained utility measurable from the full output distribution and provides a leave-one-out retraining oracle for each target. From a fixed number of oracle observables, we estimate empirical target coverage, the fraction of targets reliably flagged when deleted and rarely flagged under any other deletions. To recover missed deletions, CEO further expands each observable representation into high-dimensional activation space with the frozen encoder of a SAE trained on the original model. ##### **Open CDLM: A Benchmark and Library for Continuous Diffusion Language Models**
2611.27536v1 by Hao Jiang, Ruixi Chen, Jiayi Han, Yulei Sun, Yujian Chen, Gen Li, Brian. K Chen, Guang Cheng, Weijian Luo, Tianyang Hu

Reliable comparisons of continuous diffusion language models (CDLMs) require evaluating both model fit and the distribution of generated text. Two common practices leave this goal only partially addressed. Likelihood-based evaluation often reports a variational upper bound on discrete negative log-likelihood, whose slack can confound comparisons. Generated-sample-based evaluation commonly combines generative perplexity with within-sequence unigram entropy; both scores can remain favorable even when a sampler produces the same text on every draw. We introduce \textbf{Open CDLM}, a benchmark for evaluating CDLMs and their samplers, and describe the design of an accompanying open-source library to support reproducible comparisons. For likelihood-based evaluation, we clarify which comparisons of model fit are supported by reported perplexity bounds and how those conclusions depend on the evaluation procedure. For sample-based evaluation, we use \textit{Kernel Text Discrepancy} (\textbf{KTD}) to compare collections of generated and real texts, alongside diagnostics for diversity, validity, and coverage. In controlled OpenWebText experiments, replacing a collection of texts with copies of a single selected passage improves both conventional generation scores. MAUVE detects the resulting collapse, and its response to other perturbations depends on the text representation and the type of perturbation. This work marks the beginning of Open CDLM as an open-source project for CDLM research. We will maintain and extend the library over time and welcome contributions from the community.

##### **How Many Bytes Has a BPE Model Seen? Estimating Byte-Token Exposure in Pretraining from Corpus Statistics and Embedding Norms**
2611.27506v1 by Liwei Zhang, ZhiMing Tong, Mochen Guan, Linzhe Li

Byte-level BPE vocabularies retain 256 single-byte fallback tokens. When converting such a model into a pure byte-level model, these tokens provide the starting embeddings. How much training have they received? We estimate byte-token exposure in Qwen3.8 using two independent routes. First, we tokenize 540 MB of public corpora covering 40 domains and 33 languages, then combine per-domain token shares with assumed pretraining mixtures. Single-byte tokens account for 13 to 36% of tokens, mostly ASCII; genuine UTF-8 fallback bytes (0x80-0xFF) account for 0.15 to 0.27% under mainstream mixtures, or about 2 billion occurrences per trillion pretraining tokens. Fallback rates vary by four orders of magnitude across languages, reflecting how well each script is covered by the vocabulary. Second, we analyze embedding and output-matrix row norms for four open models, using untrained padding rows as a zero-exposure baseline and ordinary tokens with known frequencies for calibration. In all four models, the 13 bytes that cannot occur in valid UTF-8 remain at initialization, while every legal high byte lies in a high-frequency band. For Qwen3.8, the norm-based estimate agrees with the corpus estimate within a factor of four. The norm-frequency relation eventually folds, so inversion is useful only as a model-specific, order-of-magnitude estimate. No private data were used.

##### **The Assistant Persona Differs Across Languages in Activation Space**
2611.27504v1 by Daniel Yoo, Naama Rozen, Evan Harris

Large language models behave differently across languages, and are noticeably less safe in lower-resource languages. This gap is well established behaviorally, but not mechanistically. Moreover, each language's assistant persona direction and its magnitude are correlated with the language's resource level. ##### **Turnout: Adaptive Attention Switching for Efficient Reasoning**
2611.27500v1 by Anna Kuzina, Mart van Baalen, Paul N. Whatmough, Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi

Post-hoc transformer linearization replaces the key-value cache of full attention, which grows linearly with generation length, with a fixed size state. This change reduces memory consumption but often hurts accuracy. In reasoning tasks, which require long generations, this effect is especially pronounced. In this work we introduce Turnout, a method that mitigates accuracy loss as a result of linearization, while maintaining its memory-reduction benefits. We apply post-hoc linearization to a pretrained full-attention model, training a linear path through distillation while leaving the original full-attention path unchanged. During response generation, the resulting hybrid model retains both paths and uses a learned policy to control when each layer switches from full to linear attention. We train the policy with reinforcement learning using a reward that balances answer correctness and memory use. Empirical evaluation of our method shows that the learned adaptive policy achieves a better accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier on reasoning tasks compared to fixed switching and static hybrid baselines.

2611.27491v1 by Jonathan von Rad, Pontus Stenetorp, Yao Lu

The EU Artificial Intelligence Act (AI Act) imposes detailed obligations on providers of high-risk AI systems but offers limited operational guidance, particularly when these systems integrate third-party General-Purpose AI (GPAI) models. This paper introduces a toolkit that fosters the development of legal-by-design AI-assisted recruitment systems by translating the core legal obligations of the AI Act's conformity assessment---specifically Articles 9--15 and Annex IV---into techno-normative requirements and corresponding guiding questions. While recruitment pipelines may involve components with varying technical complexity and differing levels of algorithmic transparency, this paper focuses on the most challenging case: the integration of third-party GPAI models into critical stages of the selection process, including resume and job description parsing, applicant scoring, and shortlist generation. This scenario presents significant challenges for downstream providers of high-risk AI systems, as it underscores the difficulties in obtaining the technical information needed to demonstrate compliance with the AI Act's techno-normative requirements, both prior to and during market deployment. After introducing the toolkit and illustrating its application through a simplified example, the paper explores informational dependencies between upstream and downstream GPAI model providers. These dependencies may produce informational gaps across the AI supply chain, potentially hindering the collection of evidence necessary for the AI system provider to ensure legal compliance. The resulting lack of technical transparency negatively affects the deployer, who may struggle to maintain meaningful oversight of the system, and ultimately impacts the persons subject to the AI output.

##### **Mechanism Parity for Simulation-Based Validation of Autonomous-Driving Perception**
2611.27447v1 by Danial Safaei, Siddartha Khastgir, Matthew D. Higgins, Jeroen Ploeg, Mohsen Alirezaei, Son Tong, Paul A. Jennings, Xingyu Zhao

Simulation is central to intelligent-vehicle validation, but visual realism or output agreement between a real scene and its synthetic counterpart does not prove that a perception system used the same evidence in both domains. This paper addresses that mechanism-parity gap for paired real-synthetic autonomous-driving perception. It unifies Decisive-Feature Fidelity, which compares decisive evidence maps in a frozen system under test (SUT), with Concept-Decisive Feature Fidelity, which projects decisive evidence into semantic scene concepts for diagnosis. The framework is a validation diagnostic rather than a standalone safety certificate: it asks whether output-consistent simulation also preserves the mechanism by which the perception model reaches that output. The empirical evidence comprises ten retrained diffusion ControlNet generators evaluated on a deterministic exact-geometry KITTI-Virtual KITTI 2 subset. Generated counterparts use a 1248-by-376-pixel padded inference canvas and are cropped back to the exact KITTI dimensions before evaluation. The final benchmark reports target-image, output-consistency, and mechanism metrics recomputed from a single audited output set, then adds metric-disagreement cases, bootstrap uncertainty, threshold sensitivity, and concept/cache integrity audits. Across the benchmark, output and appearance fidelity are at most weakly and inconsistently rank-correlated with decisive-mechanism distance for all three frozen SUTs, and a constructed positive control shows the estimator detects a known planted mechanism that output agreement misses, supporting decisive-evidence diagnostics for intelligent-vehicle simulation validation. All numerical claims are restricted to the declared exact-geometry benchmark, ten generators, and three frozen SUTs.

##### **Structurally Different, Functionally Persistent: Capability Regions Under Model Optimization**
2611.27445v1 by Jiwoo Kum, Chanwoo Kim, Doyun Kim, Seonghyeon Cho, Sihyeon Lee, Chanjun Park

Large language model (LLM) capabilities have been associated with localized parameter regions, enabling targeted model editing and intervention. However, it remains unclear whether a region identified before model optimization remains an effective intervention target afterward. We evaluate the functional persistence and parameter overlap of coding capability regions across three instruction-tuned models under quantization, pruning, and LoRA-based adaptation. Although re-extracted regions exhibit substantial parameter reorganization, ablating the original region after optimization eliminates or nearly eliminates coding performance in almost all conditions, while magnitude-matched controls preserve performance. Partition analysis further shows that the shared subset of the original and re-extracted regions accounts for most of the observed ablation effect in the majority of conditions. These results demonstrate that parameter overlap and functional persistence are distinct: capability regions can remain causally effective intervention targets even when their parameter realization changes substantially.

##### **Silent Arbitration: How Large Language Models Resolve Co-Authoritative Conflicting Instructions**
2611.27444v1 by Sihyeon Lee, Jiwoo Kum, Jihun Song, Chanwoo Kim, Chanjun Park

Large language models increasingly receive high-level instructions about roles, values, interests, and communication style. Such instructions can conflict even when a prompt assigns them equal authority and provides no priority rule. We study co-authoritative persona conflict, in which a single prompt jointly assigns two opposing personas. Across eight conflict types grounded in published source material and five LLMs, we measure conflict acknowledgment, resolution strategy, and directional stance. Models acknowledge incompatibility in 72% of direct checks but explicitly surface it in only 22% of task responses, revealing silent arbitration. Dominance accounts for 45% of dual-persona responses, and 35% of compromise-labeled responses retain directional lean. Prompt framing further changes the visible form of arbitration substantially more than ordinary paraphrase variation. These findings show that evaluating instruction following should assess whether models disclose conflicts, how they resolve them, and which persona their responses favor.

##### **Learning When to Ask: Advisor-Aware Post-Training for SLM Agents**
2611.27443v1 by Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina

Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for cheap, high-throughput agentic deployment, but they remain weak at multi-step reasoning and tool use that long-horizon tasks demand. We study how to post-train an SLM to seek advice from a stronger model and act on that advice itself, following an advisor-executor pattern. Out of the box, this pattern fails across open- and closed-source small-model families: wiring an SLM to an advisor is a harness-design matter that is not enough on its own. A post-training recipe is needed to teach the model when to ask, in a cost-aware fashion, and how to follow advice across steps. Our method, ADAPT (ADvisor-Aware Post-Training), is a multi-stage recipe that instills this behavior, learning entirely from the policy's own rollouts supervised by the environment's outcome, with no learned reward model or human annotation. Across three domains, compared to an untrained harness baseline, ADAPT improves success rate in embodied text reasoning (TextWorld) from 24.5% to 77.3%, code generation (LiveCodeBench) from 21.8% to 56.0%, and deep web search (GAIA) from 17.5% to 49.3%, while querying the advisor sparingly (~1.0-2.1 calls per task). On the two long-horizon domains it closes 70-85% of the gap to an oracle that perfectly times when to ask for advice, and on code generation it exceeds that oracle. The gains persist when the advisor is replaced by one unseen in training, showing that the learned help-seeking policy is robust to a changing advisor rather than tied to the one it was trained with.

##### **THRIFTY: Cost-Constrained Reasoning-Data Synthesis for Small Language Models**
2611.27436v1 by Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina

Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for efficient, specialized deployment, and are typically trained on reasoning trajectories distilled from a larger teacher LLM. Generating this synthetic data is a major cost of the pipeline, especially when the teacher is a frontier model. Existing collection methods, however, are largely cost-unaware, or at best spread a fixed sampling budget uniformly across queries. Such allocation ignores the wide variation in problem difficulty and the strategic over- or under-sampling it invites. Thus, we propose THRIFTY, a Thompson-sampling bandit controller that spends a fixed generation-token budget to collect diverse correct reasoning trajectories, casting the task as a bandits-with-knapsacks problem with a novel diversity-aware objective. It tracks each problem's success probability with an online Beta posterior and spends each token where it expects the most new distinct correct solutions. Because repeated solutions add progressively less value, THRIFTY adaptively stops resampling saturated problems, concentrates budget on under-covered ones, and retires the unsolvable at the budget's opportunity cost. On math (AIME, MATH-500) and code (LiveCodeBench) benchmarks, THRIFTY gathers up to 22% more diverse correct trajectories per generation token than both the standard uniform-sampling recipe and the strongest adaptive baseline, with the largest gains in the scarce-budget and hard-problem regimes.

2611.27425v1 by Ahaan Dabholkar, Harshvardhan Patel, Michalis Polychronakis, Saurabh Bagchi

##### **Jagged Robustness: Uneven Safety Transfer and Emerging Vulnerabilities under LLM Quantization**
2611.27424v1 by Ramneek Agnihotri, Yingte Xu, Hans Vath

Moreover, we verify the degradation of model safeguards by observing increased compliance with unsafe requests under quantization, and demonstrate universal adversarial suffix attacks that are benign for the full model but jailbreak quantized models. In single-task evaluation, WePM-4B attains the best average score over 30 embedding, 39 generation, and seven typed-decision benchmarks (72.86, 62.41, 61.11). Against the separate-model stack with the highest HCD, WePM achieves slightly higher balanced performance with 61.9% less resident weight memory, a 2.64-fold higher HCD.

摘要：

##### **WaXup: Weight Averaging of Experts as Data Augmentation for Robust Introspection**
2611.27353v1 by Marc Boëlle, Lucas Dixon, Alexandre Ramé

As the capabilities of large language models keep improving, traditional mechanistic interpretability methods struggle to keep pace. In contrast, trainable introspection methods emerge as a promising alternative; they repurpose the model itself as a self-diagnostic tool, trained to verbalize what it has learned during finetuning in natural language. While training on datasets of finetuned experts achieves encouraging results, it currently lacks robustness and generalization. Building on the data augmentation framework of MiXup, we introduce WaXup, a method that augments the introspection training set through linear interpolation in the weight space. This is motivated by the linear mode connectivity property: weight averaged experts interpolate the behaviors of the initial experts. We evaluate WaXup on three benchmarks: hidden-behavior models, a meta-dataset of real-world NLP tasks and AuditBench, a challenging alignment auditing suite of models adversarially trained to conceal their behaviors. WaXup improves the accuracy of generated descriptions as well as robustness to various distribution shifts. We hope to improve the reliability of trainable introspection methods towards better and safer models.

摘要：

##### **PAVE: A Harness for Geometrically Consistent Road Network Modeling with Vision-Language Models**
2611.27352v1 by Yajun Liu, Jinxi Wu, Juan Pablo Millán Raya, Xuesong Zhou

Transportation models, whether they simulate traffic or support planning, are only as reliable as the road networks they run on, down to the lanes and turns at each intersection. Practitioners still take such a network from GIS files and supply its junction and movement data by hand, a task that recurs with each new base year and design alternative, or they generate it from open map data. OpenStreetMap's lane tags give the correct layout for only 13 of the 44 approaches we test; open map data are often incomplete or inaccurate. Satellite and street-level imagery, in contrast, cover all 44 and show their lanes as painted. Vision-language models (VLMs) can read such imagery, but asked directly they lose geometric consistency when the same lane must be matched across a satellite crop, street-level photos taken from different poses and an ID-labelled network. We therefore present PAVE (Perceive, Anchor, Verify, Edit), a harness in which code measures what can be measured, traffic engineering knowledge enters as general rules and checks, and the model makes only the open judgements, each anchored to network IDs so that checks catch answers that contradict a measurement, a second reading or a traffic rule. Scored against human labels at signalised intersections, PAVE raises the share of exactly correct lane layouts from 49-64% with a single request to 86-91% for two VLMs, with most of the gain coming from reading one approach at a time on traced lanes. The same division of work edits a real network to a planning brief: the model names the links and nodes of each part of a facility, and code builds the edit from a design template. Of 18 edits, 16 pass every network check, against none when the model writes its own code, and 2 contain a design or coding error, against 7. An analysis shows where VLMs lose geometric consistency and which check catches each failure.

摘要：

##### **GRACE: Controlled Evidence Interventions for Diagnosing and Improving Knowledge Integration in GraphRAG**
2611.27350v1 by Minh Pham, Quang Nguyen, Phong Kieu, Cong Tran

GraphRAG combines a language model's parametric knowledge with retrieved graph evidence, yet aggregate accuracy cannot show whether failures stem from missing paths, distractors, or conflicts with model knowledge. We introduce GRACE, a benchmark built by executing LC-QuAD 2.0 queries over Wikidata and applying four controlled interventions to each evidence subgraph: severing a support path, adding type-compatible distractors, attaching high-degree hubs, and replacing the supported answer with a counterfactual entity. Questions and option positions remain fixed across the five states. The validated benchmark contains 4,028 questions and 20,140 question-graph pairs. These comparisons reveal that question-only routing cannot distinguish evidence states, while relevance guidance can improve answerable states yet hurt when the support path is severed: relevant facts may not jointly suffice to answer. We propose QCFSE-SA, which combines typed local attention, global graph attention, and question-conditioned evidence guidance with a calibrated graph-support gate. With the factual context and generator held fixed, it improves controlled-state accuracy from 57.17% to 62.78% (+5.61 points; question-clustered 95% CI [+5.04, +6.17]) and broken-state accuracy from 55.41% to 67.85%. Over-abstention on semantic distractors remains an open challenge.

摘要：

##### **EcomWriteBench: A Real-World Benchmark for E-commerce Writing Tasks**
2611.27349v1 by Kaicheng Liang, Jingyi Cui, Hongwei Wen, Feng Wang, Simiu Gu

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used throughout e-commerce workflows to generate content that informs purchasing decisions and supports customer interactions. Evaluating these systems requires accounting for user needs, product evidence, structural constraints, and business requirements that are not fully captured by general-purpose writing benchmarks. We introduce EcomWriteBench, a real-world benchmark containing 3,861 queries from 69 e-commerce writing tasks across 13 categories and five languages. Through task-level extraction, cross-task consolidation, and refinement guided by human feedback, EcomWriteBench constructs a shared pool of 37 abilities and identifies the subset applicable to each task. Ability-wise scoring enables us to compare state-of-the-art LLMs on EcomWriteBench and characterize their relative strengths and weaknesses across fine-grained e-commerce writing abilities. We further propose a preference-calibrated weight-learning framework that converts human pairwise preferences into interpretable scoring rubrics through global, category-level, and task-level aggregation weights. This framework provides a reusable approach for translating human preferences into automated evaluation by combining ability discovery, ability-wise scoring, and preference-calibrated aggregation, and can potentially be applied to a broad range of benchmarks beyond EcomWriteBench. On held-out queries, our scoring framework achieves 88.96% agreement with human preferences, compared with 78.54% for conventional LLM-as-a-judge scoring.

摘要：

##### **Breaking the Long Horizon: Short-Horizon Process Supervision for Long-Form Generation in LLMs**
2611.27348v1 by Heyang Xu, HanWen Hao, Qianren Mao, Zhuo Liu, Qili Zhang, Sheng Yang, Jianxin Li

Long-form generation is increasingly important for agentic LLM systems, yet current reinforcement learning methods typically optimize an entire document with a single outcome-level reward. This delayed and coarse-grained supervision makes it difficult to localize errors and assign credit across semantically distinct parts of the output. To address this, we propose a short-horizon framework that reformulates long-form generation through semantic decomposition into segment-level decisions. Each segment is generated with prefix-conditioned context, allowing cross-segment coherence to be preserved while keeping supervision local. The generated segments are then scored with rubric-based rewards for adherence, coherence, and relevance, enabling explicit local supervision at the segment level. These local rewards are used to compute step-wise advantages, yielding localized policy updates. To isolate the effect of supervision granularity, we instantiate both outcome-level supervision and our short-horizon process reward method (SH-PRM) under the same GRPO framework. Across multiple LLMs and two long-form generation benchmarks, SH-PRM consistently outperforms outcome-level supervision and strong inference-time baselines under the same optimizer and comparable budgets. Further analyses suggest that these gains primarily arise from improved credit assignment. Overall, our results suggest that aligning supervision granularity with the semantic structure of long-form generation is an effective design principle for long-form LLM training.

摘要：

##### **Learning Interaction Dynamics via Motion-Region Guidance for Robotic Video World Models**
2611.27344v1 by Yongkang He, Xiaofen Xing, Xiangmin Xu

Action-conditioned video world models imagine future observations under candidate robot actions, providing a promising visual tool for robot policy evaluation and interaction simulation. However, existing video world models often optimize global reconstruction errors instead of emphasizing interaction-relevant regions. As a result, they may preserve overall scene appearance but overlook local interaction details, leading to physically implausible outcomes such as hallucinated contact effects and inconsistent robot-object interactions. We introduce MoReWorld, a unified motion-region-guided framework that uses a shared motion region, the part of the environment that moves in response to the robot's actions, to focus dynamics learning and video generation on interaction-relevant regions. The same motion region guides local motion prediction, region-aware supervision, and motion-gated future-feature conditioning. At inference time, the motion region is predicted as a motion probability map from visual history and robot actions, without requiring future observations or ground-truth motion labels. On WorldSimProbe, MoReWorld achieves the highest overall simulator-faithfulness score, with the strongest performance on local consistency, global consistency, and interaction dynamics. These results indicate that motion-region guidance helps the model preserve scene-level consistency while more faithfully predicting action-conditioned interaction dynamics.

摘要：

##### **Unimodal Shortcuts and Joint Scene-Intent Dependence in Vision-Language Safety Representations**
2611.27339v1 by Guijia Zhang, Hongbo Qiu, Guohao Cai, Hao Zheng, Jiajun Zha, Hanqing Wang, Zhenhao Zhang, Huihao Jing, Shing-Chi Cheung, Haoxuan Che, Harry Yang

The safety of a request to a vision-language model often depends on the image: asking how to serve a dish is harmless for fresh food and harmful for spoiled food. High accuracy on situational safety benchmarks does not tell us whether a model's safety representation encodes both the scene and the user's intent, because a benchmark can often be solved from one modality alone. Studying frozen vision-language safety representations, we audit five public situational benchmarks and find that each admits a unimodal shortcut: images alone predict the labels, and on same-image prompt pairs a probe that never sees the photograph reaches 0.9645 AUROC. We therefore build a controlled evaluation that crosses safe and hazardous scenes with observation and action requests, so that no single-modality rule can exceed 0.500 group-ranking accuracy. On this control, linear probes on intermediate states of Qwen3-VL-8B and SmolVLM2-2.2B reach 0.958 and 0.930, showing that the scene-intent conjunction is linearly decodable; concatenating separately encoded image and text states already recovers most of this signal, so decodability is not evidence of a fusion mechanism. The same probes screen cheaply but generalize poorly to unseen images and other benchmarks. Reliable multimodal safety evaluation therefore needs controls that rule out unimodal shortcuts before accuracy is read as evidence of joint understanding.

摘要：

##### **Reasoning in Fewer Tokens: Concept-Normalized Multilingual Scratchpads and Reversible Model-Adaptive Reasoning Codes**
2611.27326v1 by Rabimba Karanjai, Suryabhan Singh Hada, Hemanth Hegadehalli Madhavarao, Qun Gu, Libin N. George, Wenhuan Sun, Neha Monga, Richard Williamson, Prakhar Mehrotra

Chain-of-thought prompting significantly improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) but incurs high inference costs due to lengthy autoregressive decoding. While previous efficiency approaches reduce costs by discarding tokens or utilizing continuous latent representations, these lossy methods fundamentally sacrifice interpretability and auditability. In this work, we introduce a novel compression framework for intermediate reasoning. We apply Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE) to reasoning corpora to learn a compressed vocabulary of Private Use Area (PUA) tokens, each representing frequent semantic spans. After expanding the LLM's vocabulary, we fine-tune the model via strictly 1:1, our method is lossless. We evaluate our approach on Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-14B, and Qwen2.5-32B across four diverse benchmarks. Our method reduces total generated tokens by 14.7% on average with reasoning trace lengths dropping by up to 27.1%, while incurring a negligible average accuracy cost of 1.6 percentage points. Crucially, this approach is orthogonal to existing lossy methods, allowing practitioners to stack lossless and irreversible compression techniques. We demonstrate that a compressed intermediate language provides a highly efficient computational resource that accelerates inference while fully preserving the transparency of LLM reasoning.

摘要：

##### **SAVIOR: Sample-efficient Adaptation of Vision-Language Models for OCR Representation**
2611.27319v1 by Akshata A Bhat, Sharath Naganna, Saiful Haq, Prashant Khatri, Krishna Chaitanya Reddy Tamataam, Neha Arun, Niyati Chhaya, Pushpak Bhattacharyya

Optical Character Recognition (OCR) pipelines and vision-language models exhibit high accuracy on standard OCR and document-understanding benchmarks, yet they systematically underperform on document patterns that are operationally critical in financial workflows. These patterns include vertical or rotated text, logo-embedded vendor names, fine-print regulatory clauses, degraded scans, and complex multi-column layouts. While underrepresented in public datasets, they constitute a substantial portion of real-world failure cases. We introduce SAVIOR, a sample-efficient data curation methodology that targets such high-impact failure scenarios to adapt vision-language models for robust financial OCR. We further propose PaIRS, a structure-aware evaluation metric that measures layout fidelity by comparing pairwise spatial relationships between tokens. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct with SAVIOR-Train achieves a word recall of 0.93. When the resulting layout-aware OCR output is used as context for document question answering, it attains an F1 score of 0.89, outperforming both open- and closed-source models such as GPT-4o, Mistral-OCR, PaddleOCR-VL, and DeepSeek-OCR.

摘要：

##### **Do Language Models Know Which Words Steer Their Reasoning?**
2611.27317v1 by Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Jey Han Lau, Krista A. Ehinger, Rena Gao Wei

While chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits intermediate reasoning steps from language models, it is unclear which words in the context actually steer the CoT. We identify Phantom Context Influence (PCI): language models are disproportionately steered by semantically irrelevant context words, even as the same models deny those words' relevance when asked. To localize PCI, we introduce Leave-One-Out Word JSD analysis, which removes one context word at a time from the otherwise intact context to isolate its marginal effect on the next-token distribution; words whose removal flips the prediction (pivot words) are passed to the same LLM to self-judge whether it recognizes this predictive dependence. Across four models and three benchmarks, language models largely do not know which words steer their reasoning, a mismatch we call the Generation-Judgment Contradiction. Inspired by this phenomenon, we propose Online KV Suppression: it scores every prompt word's influence over the whole chain of thought in a single pass, then zeroes the KV-cache entries of the highest-influence word the model itself judges inert and redecodes. This single-word intervention improves CoT accuracy across four models and five reasoning benchmarks, by up to 8.9 percentage points on math reasoning benchmark.

#### Abstracts
##### **TEA-Bench-BR: Can Coding Agents Turn a Branching Clinical Specification into Correct Code?**
2611.27895v1 by Marcio Andre de Souza Estevam

Coding agents increasingly implement rules written by domain experts, and bugs in such code are silent: it runs and returns a plausible answer. We introduce TEA-Bench-BR, a benchmark built on the Brazilian pathway for early autism (TEA) screening: three fictitious instruments with the structure of real ones, a multi-professional referral flow, and export to FHIR R4 with the national RNDS profiles, all specified in Portuguese. A generator samples each task's parameters (144 tasks, 6,798 hidden cases), and the hidden cases kill all 1,955 non-equivalent mutants of the reference. With Gemma 4 as the agent, the 12B solves 38.0% of single-instrument tasks and none at the FHIR level; the 31B solves 77.8%, 85.2% and 78.7% at levels 1 to 3 and passes over 99% of the hidden cases, its remaining failures mostly in input validation rather than clinical decisions. With the same tasks in English, the 31B solves 57.4% at level 3, as its code validates an identifier less often; the 12B's, more often. The 12B misses 10.10% of the cases that call for referral; the 31B, 0.02%. A contamination probe on a modified PHQ-9 finds no error that follows the original rules.

摘要：

##### **LesionAgent: Training-Free Agentic Tumor Tracking and Response Assessment in Longitudinal CT**
2611.27876v1 by Maximilian Rokuss, Jeya Maria Jose Valanarasu, Alejandro Lozano, Benjamin Hamm, Yannick Kirchhoff, Fabian Isensee, Klaus Maier-Hein

Every few months, a cancer patient's CT scans decide whether treatment continues. 3D foundation models can now detect, segment, register and track lesions, yet none of them reads the scans as a radiologist does. A radiologist decides which findings are lesions, which are the same lesion months later and whether a new spot means progression, and checks each decision against the extensive rules of a clinical guideline. We introduce LesionAgent, an LLM agent that performs this read end to end without task-specific training. It operates frozen 3D models as tools, acts in 3D through coordinate-labelled views, remembers every lesion across visits in a registry, verifies every model output it relies on, and leaves every number to a deterministic guideline engine. On four public longitudinal CT datasets, LesionAgent tracks more lesions and measures them more accurately than every automatic baseline, and where the same 3D models chained by fixed rules call progression falsely at 33 of 56 follow-ups, it does so twice. On clinical follow-up CTs, it agrees with the radiologists' response at 20 of 23 follow-ups, including every change in response, and without training on DeepLesion, it retrieves more of its lesions than every tracker trained on it (89.0% vs. 87.4%). Its harness, not its LLM, makes the difference: given the same LLM and 3D models but not the harness, Claude Code reaches a detection F1 of 41% and LesionAgent 78%, and an open-weight LLM that can run inside the hospital rises from 6% to 73%. LesionAgent and a napari reading app for any LLM are available at https://github.com/MIC-DKFZ/LesionAgent.

摘要：

##### **SLP-Bench: Evaluating and Improving Acoustic Claims of Reasoning Audio Language Models on Clinical Speech**
2611.27810v1 by Côme Hosxe, Rachid Riad, Quentin Spinat

Audio language models (ALMs) can answer clinical questions from speech, outperforming baselines built on handcrafted acoustic features. Reasoning ALMs generate a chain of thought that refers to properties of the speaker's voice before predicting. Each statement the model makes about the voice needs to be checked against clinicians' perceptual ratings of the same recording. On clinical speech, existing evaluations score only the final answer. We introduce Speech Language Pathologist Bench (SLP-Bench), a benchmark grading each acoustic claim in the chain of thought against clinicians' perceptual ratings of the same recording. It covers five clinician-rated datasets and 25 perceptual dimensions, and assesses claims for factuality, stability under resampling, and consistency across reworded and different questions. Across ten open-weight reasoning ALMs evaluated zero-shot, claim balanced accuracy ranges from 0.52 to 0.58, claims change under resampling and rewording, and the most consistent models are not the most factual. Fine-tuning a 7B ALM with GRPO on a reward for claims that agree with clinicians raises its claim balanced accuracy from 0.54 to 0.60 and its mean kappa from 0.39 to 0.61, while preserving answer accuracy on per-dimension questions. SLP-Bench is available upon request, subject to approval by the providers of each dataset.

摘要：

##### **A Benchmark Protocol and Fairness Analysis Towards Open-Set EEG Seizure Type Classification**
2611.27783v1 by Targol Bakhtiarvand, Terrance E. Boult, Jugal Kalita, Adham Atyabi

Automated EEG seizure type classification systems are typically trained and evaluated under the closed-set assumption, where all seizure types encountered at deployment are seen during training. In clinical practice, this assumption fails: patients present with rare or atypical seizure types that fall outside the training label space, and a system that forces a wrong label is worse than one that abstains. We introduce the first open-set recognition protocol for EEG seizure type classification on the Temple University Hospital Seizure Corpus (TUSZ v2.0.3), defining known and unknown classes using the clinical taxonomy itself: seizure types with sufficient subject representation (at least 12 subjects) form the known set, and rare types form the unknown set. We evaluate seven post-hoc open-set scorers (MSP, MaxLogit, energy, Mahalanobis, kNN, GHOST, and COSTARR) applied to a partially fine-tuned EEG Foundation Model (EFM), using a subject-disjoint leave-one-class-out (LOCO) protocol over three seeds that tests whether methods can detect seizure types excluded from fine-tuning. On true unknown classes, the best scorer (Mahalanobis) reaches a mean AUROC of 0.66, and differences among the top scorers are within seed-to-seed variation. Held-out known seizure types from unseen subjects are detected only slightly above chance (AUROC 0.54-0.58 for all scorers). Per-subject rejection rates are highly uneven for all scorers, and GHOST does not significantly improve per-subject fairness over energy scoring (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank, p >= 0.25). We release the protocol, subject splits, and code as an initial baseline; our results indicate that open-set seizure type recognition remains a hard, open problem.

摘要：

##### **Neurodegenerative Differential Diagnosis with Knowledge-enhanced Structural Reasoning**
2611.27427v1 by Mingxuan Wang, Siyuan Du, Yanfeng Wang, Ya Zhang, Chencheng Zhang, Jiangchao Yao

Accurate differential diagnosis of neurodegenerative disorders remains challenging due to overlapping clinical manifestations and heterogeneous, often incomplete multimodal observations. Existing multimodal models can integrate imaging and clinical measurements but often overlook disease-specific evidence patterns and pairwise relations among competing diagnoses. We therefore propose Knowledge-enhanced Structural Reasoning (KnowSR), which aligns multimodal patient representations with a literature-grounded knowledge bank of disease prototypes, signed evidence directions, and pairwise diagnostic relations. Specifically, in KnowSR, patient-specific knowledge alignment and target-aware pooling inject structured disease evidence, while anchored relational reasoning reconciles pairwise diagnostic margins over a sparse differential graph, thereby collectively enhancing differential diagnostic capability. Across extensive experiments, KnowSR consistently outperforms 19 baselines on patient-level six-class diagnosis under patient-disjoint five-fold evaluation. This shows that grounding patient evidence in structured medical knowledge enables more effective differential diagnosis than naive multimodal fusion alone.

摘要：

##### **TrojanMaxxing: One-shotting VLM Backdoors with Weight-Space Programming**
2611.27425v1 by Ahaan Dabholkar, Harshvardhan Patel, Michalis Polychronakis, Saurabh Bagchi

Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can reason directly about the weights of other neural networks. Given only a natural-language objective, an LLM can inspect a model's weights, understand what they compute, and edit them directly without gradient-based optimization. This capability, termed weight-space programming (WSP), has been used to improve model performance on math reasoning, medical QA and other benchmarks. We propose TrojanMaxxing, a novel strategy to backdoor vision-language models (VLMs), by reframing it as a WSP task. We use Claude Opus 5.5 and Mythos 5.2 agents to backdoor VLMs without any optimization or training methods. The agents inspect the model weights, apply targeted edits, and co-design an imperceptible visual trigger that activates the backdoor. We then study the agents' strategies and present a thorough evaluation of the trojaned models on various benchmark datasets. TrojanMaxxing achieves attack success rates of 93%-100% on content moderation and 78%-100% on OCR while maintaining clean accuracy. Perhaps most consequentially, we show that injecting backdoors in a training-free manner is possible by simply, "asking an agent to do it".

摘要：

##### **Evidence sufficiency assessment enables cost-effective review allocation in on-premise clinical AI agents**
2611.27390v1 by Yiwen Qiu, Che Sun, Shu Shen, Wentao Wang

On-premise clinical AI agents must auto-accept high-confidence diagnoses, deferring others to clinician review, yet token probabilities and self-reported confidence miss errors, while more reliable five-run consistency incurs fivefold compute. We propose training-free decision-point evidence sufficiency assessment and a three-tier sequential triage protocol. Masking reasoning and diagnosis, the same 4B model scores sufficiency from objective evidence via prior-debiased probabilities over five ordered levels. Calibration freezes a nominal top-50% threshold, selecting single-run, five-run consistency, or full-deferral tiers by whether the one-sided 95% Wilson lower confidence bound of pass rate among accepted cases meets the 95% clinical target line. Across 1,605 MIRA-v2, CDM, VivaBench, and in-hospital liver malignancy primary cohort cases, MIRA-v2 single-run top-50% pass rate reached 98.91%, matching five-run consistency, saving ~80% compute; CDM achieved 96.00% test attainment by escalating to the five-run consistency tier; cross-specialty, missing-imaging, and combined enriched cohorts entered the full-deferral tier, capping calibration false-certification rate at 0.00%-3.50%.

摘要：

##### **Can Reasoning and Image Description Enhance Yes/No Visual Mathematical Understanding?**
2611.27302v1 by Guangcheng Song, Weichuan Wang, Linqi Song

Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on general visual tasks, yet their ability to understand mathematical images remains limited. This paper investigates whether explicit reasoning and textual image descriptions can enhance VLMs' performance on binary yes/no visual mathematical questions. To achieve this, we curated a paired evaluation set to benchmark prominent open-source VLMs under varying prompt and input conditions. In particular, we leverage Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting and use textual descriptions, either replacing or augmenting visual inputs, to isolate and evaluate mathematical comprehension. Our results show that explicit reasoning improves performance, especially in paired consistency accuracy, while textual image descriptions do not consistently help and may degrade performance. These findings suggest that reasoning is important for stable visual mathematical judgement, whereas description-based intermediates require more careful design.

摘要：

##### **DermKG: A Concept-Centric Knowledge Graph for Interpretable Skin Disease Diagnosis from Dermatological Morphology**
2611.27283v1 by Cheng Huang, Yanmei Li, Mao Lin

Dermatologists describe lesions with a standardized morphological lexicon, which makes concept-based models attractive for interpretable skin disease diagnosis. How much diagnostic information these concepts actually carry, however, is rarely quantified. We present DermKG, a concept-centric knowledge graph built on the expert-annotated SkinCon subset of Fitzpatrick17k (3,230 images, 48 concepts, 113 diseases). DermKG has 3,452 nodes of 8 types and 19,365 edges of 11 relation types, and encodes disease concept profiles, concept co-occurrence, a three-level diagnostic hierarchy, and 20 clinical morphology rules, all of which are supported by the training data (15 strong, 5 moderate). An information-ceiling analysis shows that the 3,230 lesions collapse onto only 813 distinct concept vectors, bounding concept-only top-1 accuracy at 38.9\% for diseases and 77.6\% for the nine categories. Within this bound, DermKG-Net, a graph neural network that scores lesions against disease prototypes and aggregates them along the hierarchy, raises category-level balanced accuracy from 0.257 to 0.411 over the strongest of 11 baselines (paired bootstrap difference $+0.150$, 95\% CI $[0.073, 0.231]$) and more than doubles minority-category recall. Ablations show that most of this gain comes from the hierarchical prototype objective, with a smaller contribution from graph structure. DermKG further yields rule-based reasoning chains and concept-level explanations for individual predictions.

摘要：

##### **LingSante-Pulse: Unified Physiological Pretraining through Random-Feature Prediction and Cross-Modal Alignment**
2611.27206v1 by Linxiao Yang, Wenwei Wang, Tian Zhou, Bingqing Peng, Zihao Zhang, Zuyi Zhu, Jinjie Gu, Liang Sun

Clinical monitoring and wearable devices capture complementary physiological signals, but their partially overlapping modality coverage makes within-signal information and cross-modal relationships difficult to exploit jointly. We introduce LingSante-Pulse, a unified, general-purpose and flexible physiological representation framework that learns from these heterogeneous recordings. A shared, signal-conditioned encoder processes each physical channel independently and supports different numbers and combinations of signals for multimodal assessment and single-sensor inference. Pairwise cross-modal alignment, structured random-feature prediction (RFP) and masked waveform reconstruction jointly learn physiological relationships and preserve informative within-signal structure. Across 38 general physiological task configurations spanning PPG, ECG, EEG and EMG, the frozen encoder achieves the best dataset-balanced mean rank in each domain. In sleep assessment, multimodal staging on the HMC dataset and EEG-only staging on the Sleep-EDF dataset reach Macro-F1 scores of 0.7784 and 0.7941, respectively, while single-lead ECG apnea screening achieves an AUROC of 0.9183. Controlled experiments show that function prediction and waveform reconstruction provide complementary supervision for retaining temporal and rhythmic information. Representations learned under continually refreshed random supervision generalize to unseen teacher realizations, and correct auxiliary modality correspondence improves respiration-only representations. These results demonstrate that a single encoder can learn reusable physiological features across signal types, prediction targets and sensor configurations from partially overlapping recordings.

摘要：

##### **GEG-SAM: Gaze Exploration-Guided Segment Anything Model for Weakly Supervised Polyp Segmentation**
2611.27183v1 by Ao Wang, Lei Zhang, Lituan Wang, Zhenwei Zhang, Xiangbao Gong, Zhiyi Zhang, Shunyao Wang, Xianglei Yuan, Zizhou Wang

Endoscopist gaze offers an unobtrusively acquired record of lesion-oriented visual exploration during routine clinical inspection, providing relevant supervision for tasks. However, existing methods treat fixations as isolated spatial prompts directly associated with the target, overlooking the fact that natural gaze records a continuous visual search process in which fixations are temporally connected, spatially organized, and coupled with the visual content. Interpreting each fixation independently based only on its local visual content overlooks the temporal evolution and spatial organization of the gaze trajectory, whereas modeling relations among fixations alone cannot reveal the visual semantics of the regions they attend to. To this end, we propose the Gaze Exploration-Guided Segment Anything Model (GEG-SAM), a principled weakly supervised framework designed to explicitly exploit structured gaze relations and their corresponding visual cues to modulate SAM for polyp segmentation. Specifically, GEG-SAM captures structured gaze relations by modeling the temporal dynamics and spatial topology of gaze exploration. It then associates these relational representations with fixation-aligned visual context to derive gaze-conditioned tokens, which are further injected into the frozen SAM encoder to modulate its pretrained image features. In this way, GEG-SAM effectively exploits the rich information embedded in gaze exploration to guide SAM toward lesion-oriented segmentation. Extensive experiments on five public polyp segmentation datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, improving the mean Dice by 9.30 percentage points over the strongest weakly supervised baseline.

摘要：

##### **How Does SAM-3 Adapt? Capacity, Update Geometry, and Domain Transfer**
2611.27142v1 by Allan Kazakov, Abdurrahman Javat, Duygu Cakir

Promptable segmentation foundation models are increasingly used in specialized domains, but it remains unclear how SAM-3 should be fine-tuned when adapting it beyond its original training distribution. We study this question by comparing five adaptation families --LoRA, HiRA, KRAdapter, QuanTA, and Full Fine-Tuning-- across five segmentation datasets spanning medical imaging, remote sensing, and dense material segmentation. Across these datasets, compact parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods often match or approach Full Fine-Tuning in DSC and IoU, suggesting that strong in-domain adaptation does not always require updating the full model. However, this performance cannot be explained by nominal adapter rank or trainable parameter count alone. To investigate why different methods perform similarly, we analyze their update spectra and their behavior under LoveDA Urban/Rural transfer. Our SVD analysis shows that Full Fine-Tuning updates are spectrally compressible. However, successful PEFT methods do not simply recover the same update directions; instead, they often concentrate changes in different components and subspaces. The domain-transfer study further shows that methods with similar in-domain performance can respond differently under distribution shift. Together, these results suggest that SAM-3 adaptation is often capacity-saturated in practice, while competitive fine-tuning arises through method-specific update paths rather than a single low-rank approximation to Full Fine-Tuning.

摘要：

##### **Learning 3D MRI Representations with Generative Adversarial Networks**
2611.27057v1 by Sujeet Lahane, Sapna Mishra, Akash Suryavanshi, Tapan Gandhi, Bharat Biswal

Learning representations of visual images is a challenging task. It requires extracting abstract patterns from high-dimensional data without explicit supervision. This becomes pronounced with 3D MR imaging due to sparse features, higher dimensional inputs, and smaller datasets. Many state-of-the-art representation learning algorithms successfully rely on transformers and diffusion-based architectures. However, these methods struggle with smaller datasets. In effect, solution methods relax the problem by slicing 3D volumes and solving the problem for 2D medical imaging, resulting in a loss of spatial 3D information. We present R2RG, a representation learning method based on Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), with Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) honing the reconstruction task. Our key insights lie in exploiting architectural biases in 3D CNNs, combined with data-efficient auto-encoding GANs and a composite loss function to enhance reconstruction fidelity and stabilize training. Our experiments on three standard datasets and a locally collected dataset show that R2RG not only achieves superior performance but also generalizes to heterogeneous and smaller datasets. Furthermore, representations learned with R2RG outperform those learned with contrastive-based representation learning methods on Parkinson's and Alzheimer's disease classification. Our work demonstrates that reconstruction-based representation learning is a promising direction for 3D medical imaging under limited data availability.

摘要：

##### **Scalable Evaluation of Medical AI using Evidence-based Practice Recommendations**
2611.27007v1 by Teya Bergamaschi, Bijal Rajput, Akshay Goel, Matthew Thompson, Jacqueline Shreibati, Dave Steiner, Ashley Carrick, Mahvish Nagda, Dale R. Webster, Avinatan Hassidim, Katherine Chou, Yossi Matias, Po-Hsuan Cameron Chen, Christina Chen, Mike Schaekermann, Yun Liu, Anil Palepu

Rigorously evaluating medical artificial intelligence (AI) requires testing open-ended clinical management across diverse presentations and stages of care. However, existing benchmarks rely on manual expert authoring, limiting coverage across conditions, conditional care pathways, and evolving guidelines. We develop an automated pipeline that extracts and verifies structured clinical criteria and branching decision trees from practice recommendations to generate scenarios spanning the care continuum, including nuanced presentations and practice variations across regions and guideline editions. Applied to 436 BMJ Best Practice clinical monographs (354 US, 82 International), this yields 3,200 scenarios spanning differential diagnosis, investigations, treatment, and complications, plus targeted suites for regional specificity (731 US/International pairs) and temporal freshness (630 scenarios). To grade responses, we develop a clinician-calibrated autorater that scores management plans on a 4-point rubric stratifying deviations by clinical severity. Across Gemini models, scaling improves adherence, while web search (+0.8 percentage points [pp] on average) and multi-sample synthesis (+2.2 pp) combine additively (+2.8 pp). In context, full monographs consistently trail condensed excerpts. On targeted suites, web search narrows international performance gaps (+4.2 pp) and improves adherence to newly updated guidance (+4.6 pp). In a study with Primary Care Physicians (n = 9) across 200 scenarios, AI assistance raises adherence from 50.5% to 70.0%, driven largely by fewer clinical omissions. Overall, our results demonstrate a scalable evaluation setup using an evidence-based corpus originally written to guide human clinical decision-making, and demonstrate its viability to assess medical AI and its assistance on clinician decision-making.

摘要：

##### **SecretAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Privacy Protection against Vision Language Models**
2611.26936v1 by Minda Zhao, Yue Zhang, Mengyu Wang

Vision-language models (VLMs) can infer private information from shared images, including locations, personally identifiable information (PII), and personal attributes such as age and sex, even from medical images. Adversarial perturbations can protect such information against black-box VLMs, but existing methods overlook image quality and introduce visible artifacts into the images they protect. Relative to the matched global-only controls, the findings and relation objectives yield consistent gains across the evaluated datasets. These results demonstrate the practical value of treating observed and unobserved report concepts differently during medical image--text representation learning.
